# Optimization log

What the compiler's cost has actually done, change by change. **This is `scale-test`'s deliverable.**

**Read the tables downwards.** Each row is one recorded change, dated. A column *is* its history, so
the progression is the thing you see rather than something you have to reconstruct.

`scale-test` renders **no verdict**. There is nothing to pass, no golden to accept, and no light to
turn green — it is an instrument, and this document is what it is an instrument *for*. The last row
of each table below is the previous datapoint: every run reads it and reports the delta from it, so
you still see exactly what moved, without anything failing.

## How rows get here

Rows are **appended by the tool, not by hand**. Running

```
maxon-shv2 scale-test --note="<what changed, and why the numbers moved>"
```

records this run as a new dated row in both tables. **A run without `--note` records nothing** —
scale-test is run dozens of times a day against dirty trees and abandoned experiments, and a log of
all of them would be a log nobody reads.

**`--note` is an instruction, not a request: if you pass one, the row is written.** Even if the
numbers are identical to the row above it. The run will tell you plainly that nothing moved, and then
record it anyway, because you are the one who knows whether it is a datapoint and the instrument is
not.

The `--note` is not a formality. The instrument can see exactly *what* moved and can never see *why*,
and six months from now a number with no reason attached is worth almost nothing. So the reason is
demanded at the one moment it is still known: from you, now. See
[`maxon-shv2/Testing/ScaleHistory.maxon`](../maxon-shv2/Testing/ScaleHistory.maxon).

## Reading the numbers

The rungs are generated programs, each **double** the last, so rung 5 is 32× rung 0. Rung 0 shows
constant-factor wins; only rung 5 shows whether a change bent the curve.

**And because the ladder doubles, the RATIO between two rungs IS the growth.** Divide a rung by the
one before it: **2× is linear, 4× is quadratic**. That is the whole method — there is nothing fitted,
no exponent, no residual, and no threshold for anyone to argue about. `scale-test` prints that ratio
beside every phase; these tables carry the raw counts it is taken from.

| what | how much to trust it |
| --- | --- |
| **allocations, bytes** (per rung) | **Exact and bit-for-bit reproducible.** The same source through the same compiler makes exactly the same allocations, on an idle machine or a loaded one. A number here that moved, moved for a *reason*. |
| **the rung-over-rung ratio** | **Exact**, for the same reason: it is a division of two numbers that cannot move. |
| **time** | **Not measured, and never will be here.** It is machine-dependent, so a dated column of it would compare a loaded box in July against an idle one in August. For the milliseconds of one compile, use the compiler's own `--log=compiler:debug`. |

These are the compiler's own allocation counts and byte volumes while compiling that rung — **not**
peak resident memory, which nothing currently measures.

> ### ⚠ TWO READING RULES SINCE 2026-07-14, both from the Go-growth / raw-counter change:
>
> **1. ×2.1 — not ×2.00 — is the byte column's LINEAR reading.** `Array` grows by Go's `nextslicecap`
> (double below 256 elements, then ease toward 1.25×), and that ratio is a *function of the buffer's
> size*, so the bytes a buffer allocates over its life are a *rising* multiple of its final size (2.0 at
> the threshold, up to 5.0 as the ratio approaches 1.25). The ladder doubles the program each rung, so
> that multiple climbs each rung, and **a phase whose bytes are perfectly linear reads above ×2.00 while
> it climbs.** It saturates at ×5 — a transient bend, not a superlinearity. So a byte reading around
> ×2.1 is this policy; well past it is still a quadratic. See `grownCapacity` in `stdlib/Array.maxon`.
>
> **2. The `allocs` column counts the RAW allocator layer, and older rows do not.** Every row on or below
> the "three changes rebased onto…" row counts raw+tracked allocations; every row above counts only
> tracked (structs, handles, Strings), so array/string *buffers* were invisible to it. The two are not
> comparable — the one-time step up (~1.06M at rung 5) is the instrument gaining sight of allocations
> that were always there, not the compiler doing more.

Frees are measured and reported but not tabulated here: they track allocations almost exactly, so a
third table would be a near-duplicate paid for in width. They are in `--result-json` for anyone who
wants them.

Dates are UTC. The columns assume a six-rung ladder; changing the ladder's length changes what the
numbers mean, so the tool refuses to append a row of a different width rather than make the tables
ragged.

The false clobbersFlags broke compare/branch fusion on `if a > 0 and b > 0`, because a short-circuit's seed literal sits between the compare and its branch: the backward scan stopped at the `mov` and refused to fuse. | 2026-07-14 | CORPUS CHANGED -- ⚠ THE DELTA COLUMN ON THIS ROW IS MEANINGLESS. The +26% is a BIGGER CORPUS, not a slower compiler; this row is the NEW BASELINE and nothing above it is comparable. ScaleCorpus was BLIND in the one dimension that mattered. The parser builds SSA on the fly at a cost of O(vars-in-scope) per branch -- each `if` SNAPSHOTS the whole mutable-var set, each `while` MINTS A PHI PER VAR IN SCOPE -- so the true cost is O(V x B). But `longFunction` and `deepBlocks` each declared ONE accumulator and mutated it in every branch: V=1, O(V x B) collapsed to O(B), and the knobs NAMED for growing branches could not see the cost of growing branches. Per user directive, FIX THE CORPUS'S REALISM, NOT THE PROBE: both knobs now carry SIX mutable locals (a fixed, realistic count -- real functions carry a handful) and each branch mutates a ROTATING SUBSET, never all of them, because a branch that writes every local makes "cost O(vars in scope)" and "cost O(vars assigned)" the same number and the corpus cannot tell apart the two things that decide whether a write-trail is worth building. deepBlocks' two arms now write DIFFERENT locals, so the merge does real work instead of the trivial one-phi case. Also: every guard is now a SHORT-CIRCUIT `and`, because the corpus contained `and`/`or` EXACTLY NOWHERE -- found the hard way when the `const` flags fix moved zero memory. WHAT IT NOW SEES, and it is the defect OPEN.md #5 predicted: pruneDeadBlockArgs ALLOCATIONS grow x1.98 x1.99 x1.995 x1.997 x1.999 -- dead linear -- while its BYTES grow x2.00 x2.02 x2.04 x2.09 x2.17. THE BYTE COLUMN CAUGHT WHAT THE ALLOC COLUMN COULD NOT, again. | 2026-07-14 | Loop phis: a loop now carries a phi for the vars its extent ASSIGNS, not every var in scope. `parseWhileStatement` minted a header phi for EVERY mutable var in scope, unconditionally, before the body was parsed -- so it could not know which vars the body touches. A function's `var`s accumulate, so the Nth loop minted ~N phis and burned a ValueId on each, INCLUDING every one pruneDeadBlockArgs later deleted. blockArgIdBound (and every dense column sized by it) is O(ValueIds), so phis nobody kept were paid for in BYTES by every pass in between. THAT is why the ALLOC column stayed dead linear while the BYTE column bent: the allocations were not more numerous, they were BIGGER. Fixed by reading the loop's assigned names off the TOKENS up front (namesAssignedIn / loopBodyEndIndex). ⚠ THE DESIGN TRAP, RECORDED BECAUSE I PROPOSED IT AND IT IS UNSOUND: minting the phi LAZILY on a var's first mention CANNOT WORK -- parseIfStatement snapshots the mutable-var set into local ValueId arrays on the Maxon CALL STACK, and a phi minted lazily after such a snapshot retroactively changes the value that was live where it was taken, with nothing able to reach in and patch it (`while c / if d / x = 2` then returns 1, not 2). EAGER minting is what makes those snapshots true, and once minting is eager the criterion is ASSIGNMENTS, not mentions -- a var the loop never assigns keeps its pre-loop value, whose definition DOMINATES the header, so a read binds it correctly with no phi at all. GOLDENS DID NOT MOVE, and that is the proof the whole cost was intermediate: pruneDeadBlockArgs and elimTrivialBlockArgs were already deleting precisely this surplus, so the emitted code was always identical -- the ValueIds were minted, carried through four passes inflating every dense column, and then thrown away. Eight sequential pressured loops minted 332 header phis of which 260 were surplus; they now mint exactly 72. specs-shv2 242/0, no E5001 (fewer dead phis means LOWER maxlive -- this is the anti-E5001 direction). | 208,898 | 410,250 | 813,602 | 1,622,912 | 3,251,282 | 6,546,044 |

| 2026-07-14 | SHIFTS: Go's rule -- the count is NOT masked, shr is ARITHMETIC, only a NEGATIVE count is an error. WHAT MOVED, and it is the ONLY thing that moved: +0.56% bytes / +0.05% allocs, LINEAR (bytes delta ratios x1.97 x1.98 x1.99 x2.00 x2.00 on a DOUBLING ladder; rung5/rung0 = 31x on a 32x ladder). WHY: the parser grew a real CONSTANT FOLDER, and it is TWO DENSE PER-VALUE COLUMNS beside valueTags -- valueConsts (ParsedInt, 8 bytes) + valueConstKnown (bool, 1 byte) = 9 bytes per SSA value, pushed in lockstep by mintValue and re-created per FUNCTION (so the cost is bounded by the largest function, not the module). It exists because the shift rule is the one rule in the language that must ask WHAT IS this operand, not WHAT TYPE is it: a negative count is E2054 and a count of 64-or-more names a different shift than the hardware performs. WHAT DID NOT MOVE, and this is the point: the GUARD. A shift by a count the compiler cannot see now emits 6-7 ops (an and/neg/or/sar mask, then a select or a count-clamp), and the corpus has NO dynamic shifts -- so the guard contributes exactly zero here, and a CONSTANT count in 0..63 (every x shr 8, every x shl 3) is folded and pays nothing at all. The curve therefore stayed clean: the delta is 9 bytes x values and nothing else, which is why it doubles when the ladder doubles. specs 2950/0 -> 2957/0 (+7), specs-shv2 268/0 -> 274/0 (+6). | 209,735 | 411,095 | 813,883 | 1,619,761 | 3,232,051 | 6,457,789 |

| 2026-07-15 | Deleted the query spine's WRITE-ONLY dependency graph (depIndex / recordDependency / clearDepsFor / activeQueryStack / QueryKey / queryKeyString). Nothing ever read a bucket: shv2 validates a memo by a content-hash compare whose inputs are named in parseMemoKey, so a dependency is DERIVED from the input, never recorded as it is read. Programs grow by adding TYPES, not by widening them, so the cost is O(accesses x 5.3) - linear with a bounded constant - and a per-layout name->index map would buy a heap Map per declared type plus a name hash per lookup to save ~2.6 byte-compares. Result, branch minus main: +148 +256 +472 +904 +1,768 +3,496, ratios x1.73 x1.84 x1.92 x1.96 x1.98 -> 2.00 on a DOUBLING ladder = LINEAR. The rung's SPLIT is confirmed exactly too: StructLayout.fieldExported (parse +111->+2,715 and signatures +24->+520) = +135 -> +3,235, and checkStructFieldTypes (resolveTypes) = +13 -> +261. phase:regalloc, phase:lex, phase:isel, phase:merge and phase:lowerMaxonToStd are EXACTLY +0 — not "small", zero. Main's own absolutes independently reproduce the wave-2 row (343,014 / 9,967,745), so the trend now has two rebuilt-from-source anchor points and the numbers above are comparable to the two rows beneath them. ⭐ WHY checkStructFieldTypes IS LINEAR IS STRUCTURAL, NOT JUST MEASURED, and that is the load-bearing half: it walks the WHOLE project's struct registry while `resolveTypes(project, module)` takes a MODULE, which is exactly the silhouette of an O(files x structs) quadratic. It is not one. PassPipeline.run dispatches each pass EXACTLY ONCE (PassPipeline.maxon:211) over the single merged maxonModule, and Compiler.maxon:167 is the ONLY PassPipeline.create in the tree — so the walk runs once per COMPILE and costs O(total declared fields), each field one interner array-index plus two hash lookups (resolveNamedType). That distinction is not academic: Compiler.maxon:130's own comment records that `queryProgramSignatures` is a whole-program query sitting UNDER a per-file one and has grown and had removed TWO quadratics of precisely that shape. checkStructFieldTypes sits in the pipeline, not under the per-file query, which is what makes it linear. Measured ratios x1.62 x1.76 x1.86 x1.93 x1.96 — approaching 2.00 FROM BELOW, the signature of a*n+b (the one MapIterator per compile is the b), never above it. ⭐ THE ONE NUMBER ABOVE 2.00, AND IT IS NOT A BEND: regalloc:rewrite BYTES reads x2.204 on the last rung while its ALLOCS read x1.997 — same allocation count, bigger allocations (bytes/alloc 29.9 29.9 30.0 30.1 30.3 33.4, one step). It is capacity rounding in geometric array growth, and the proof is that the bytes ratios BOUNCE BOTH WAYS: regalloc:blockOrder BYTES goes x2.479 at rung 2 and back DOWN to x1.988 at rung 3. A superlinear curve does not bounce back down. It is also IDENTICAL on both sides of the A/B, so it is not this rung's in any case. ALLOCATIONS are the exact, bit-reproducible column and they converge on 2.00 from below in every phase and every regalloc sub-phase. ⚠ WHAT I DELIBERATELY DID NOT CHANGE, EACH WITH A NUMBER RATHER THAN A FEELING. (1) The 0.0026% iterator: the rung's row invited the optimizer to "disagree with a NUMBER" over checkStructFieldTypes' per-layout ArrayIterator. The number is 261 allocations out of 9,971,241 at rung 5. Declined — constant factors are not the mandate, and the neighbouring loop over param types is an iterator too, so an index loop here would buy 0.0026% and cost the consistency. (2) `declareSelfFieldAliases` (Parser.maxon:5029) asks `fieldTypeOf(layout.name, ...)` PER FIELD, which re-hashes the struct name once per field when the layout is already in hand — F redundant map lookups per method entry where 1 would do. Left alone: F is max 22 / mean 4.14 across all 166 type declarations in maxon-shv2/ + stdlib/, so it is a constant factor, AND the call is load-bearing rather than a lookup — `fieldTypeOf` is the door that RE-INTERNS a `named` type into the calling file's own interner (reading the column raw would bind aliases to the SWEEP's type-name ids, which this file's interner cannot resolve). A layout-taking overload would save ~4 byte-compares and add a SECOND adoption door beside the one `emitFieldLoad` already uses. (3) `StructLayout.indexOfField`: OPEN.md #28 — READ, not re-derived. Its O(F^2) lives in a dimension this instrument holds at F=2 by construction; the debt judgment there is measured and sound and I add nothing to it. (4) Method-call resolution is O(1) per call site and has no scan: `methodCallsAt` is a 4-token lookahead plus one scope hash lookup, `requireStructBinding` is an interner array-index plus one `structOf` hash, and `qualifiedMethodName` allocates one name per call site = linear in call sites. ⚠ AND THE HONEST LIMIT, RESTATED BECAUSE IT IS THE THING A FUTURE READER WILL WANT: the instance-method mechanism ITSELF is measured at ZERO here and CANNOT be otherwise — ScaleCorpus emits types with fields and no METHODS, so no generated program declares one, calls one, or names a bare self field. Every allocation in the +148 is the FIELD-DECLARATION path. The receiver, the aliases and the E3014 check are bounded by CONSTRUCTION (reading the code), not by this ladder. Closing that knob is the instrument's rung; I did not touch the instrument. (2) The two maps are NOT one-fact-twice: they store INDEPENDENT facts (return register class vs per-parameter float mask), both rebuilt every compile from stdModule.functions, so they cannot silently drift — the shared key set is derived, not a hazard. SLICE A — exact decimal->IEEE-754 float literals. parseFloatBits applied the decimal exponent by repeated *10 in an i64, which WRAPPED: 1.0e20 gave a wrong value, 1.0e64 silently became 0.0 (10^k contains 2^k, so k>=64 is exactly 0 mod 2^64), and 1.0e63 HUNG THE COMPILER FOREVER (10^63 == 2^63 == i64.min, negative, so the shiftUp normalization loop doubled a negative without bound). NOT A PORT: v1's NumberParsing.maxon:13-226 is BYTE-IDENTICAL to shv2's and carries the identical bug (shv2 inherited it by copying); maxon-sharp sidesteps it via .NET double.Parse. COST +1,106/+2,194/+4,370/+8,722/+17,426/+34,834 rung0..5 — exact doubling per rung, LINEAR in program size, attributed exactly: rung 0 holds 278 float literals (--emit-corpus) and 1106/278 = 3.98, i.e. an exact conversion needs two heap magnitudes where the wrong i64 pair needed none, at 2 allocations per Array (Self{} envelope + first push's ensureCapacity). 0.30% of rung-0 allocations, 0.33% of rung-5's; all phase ratios unchanged x2.00-x2.11; NOT a new complexity class. The 768+sticky correctness fix itself is +2 allocs/+80 bytes FLAT at every rung. NO small-value fast path (it would be a second implementation of one fact) and none is needed: allocations are FLAT from 1 to 34 significant digits because a magnitude's width tracks its VALUE (empty array == zero), and 91% of the 1,402 float literals in shv2+stdlib carry 2-3 digits — ~1.9ms of a ~13s self-build (0.015%). Independent optimizer: NO superlinearity, NO code change. REJECTED WITH MEASUREMENTS: hoisting pow10 out of bigMulPow10's stride loop (-0.9%..-3.1% wall, ZERO allocation change, and identical code read 79,368 then 70,215 us — a 13% load swing, so the gain is BELOW THE NOISE FLOOR of the only instrument that can see it); factoring 10^k = 2^k*5^k to fold 2^k into the binary exponent (would cut operand width 1.43x and the dominant divide loop ~30%, but it is a constant factor that rewrites the exponent bookkeeping the brand-new subnormal/underflow paths depend on — bit-risk against freshly-settled correctness); Knuth algorithm D for the 53-step mantissa loop; fusing bigCompare+bigSubtract. Worst admitted input is 768 digits at e-300 (~350-400 us/literal), NOT 1000 digits at e-324 (201 us) — the latter falls below the least subnormal, significandBits clamps to 0, and the divide loop runs ZERO iterations. INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND A BLOCKER THE SUITE WAS GREEN OVER: sciExp was clamped at a FIXED 100000, but it competes with decExp in magnitudeExp = (sigDigits-1)+decExp+sciExp, and decExp moves ONE PER DIGIT — so a long enough digit run OUT-PULLS the clamp and FLIPS THE VERDICT IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. 0.<200001 zeros>1e999999999 (true magnitude 1e999799997, astronomically past f64.max) was ACCEPTED AND COMPILED TO 0.0 with no diagnostic; 1<200000 zeros>.0e-999999999 got a SPURIOUS E2011. Both verdicts confirmed against the bootstrap oracle. The ceiling is now bytes.count()+DecimalWindowSpan, swept over 96 cases spanning the cancellation region, 0 disagreements, with two regression tests PROVEN to have teeth (reintroducing the fixed clamp fails exactly those two). Review also DEDUPED the carry flush, written THREE times (bigAddSmall, bigMulSmall, and a third spelling in bigShiftLeft that pushed UNMASKED and deferred the budget check) with nothing making them agree — folded into bigPushCarry so requireLimbBudget's claim that every growing site ends there is STRUCTURAL, not conventional. And it CORRECTED THE 768 BOUND'S JUSTIFICATION: the comment claimed midpoints terminate within 752 digits, but 752 is the width of 5^1075 ALONE, ignoring the odd significand which runs to 2^54-1 and adds up to 16 more — the true widest rounding boundary (2^54-1)*2^-1075 needs EXACTLY 768, so the budget has ZERO slack while its stated reason would have justified shaving 16 digits off it (the same wrong number appeared in the spec prose: one fact written down twice, wrong both times). (1) The new phase:deadFunctions column is the whole scaling delta. (2) A FLAT instrumentation term of +2 allocs / +112 bytes per compile, identical at every rung, in three pieces, each pinned: +0/+48 billed to phase:load is the CompilePhase enum going 21->22 arms, widening six phase-count-sized 8-byte-element arrays (CompileTimings.phaseNanos, PhaseMemoryColumns allocs/frees/bytes, and two CompilePhase.allCases materializations) -- CAUSALLY PROVEN, not inferred, by building a throwaway 23rd enum arm with no pass and no probe behind it, which costs exactly +0 allocs/+48 bytes, all in phase:load, on all six rungs; +1/+32 billed INSIDE phase:deadFunctions is the phase's own PhaseProbe record (4 fields x 8B: nanos, allocTotal, allocLive, bytes), allocated after its own opening counter snapshot so it falls inside the window it opens; +1/+32 unattributed is the PhaseDelta that recordPhase builds after the closing counter read. So the pass BODY alone is +318/+136,968 ... +3,935/+6,640,884, isolated by stubbing the body and re-measuring. LINEAR, shown three ways. (a) The body's allocation FIRST DIFFERENCES double: 127, 253, 478, 930, 1829, ratios 1.992/1.889/1.946/1.967 -- the exact signature of c + k*2^n on a doubling ladder, i.e. linear plus a per-compile constant. (b) The body/sugarGate cost ratio DECREASES monotonically, 1.195->1.135->1.099->1.066->1.041->1.024 for allocs and 1.084->1.027 for bytes. sugarGate is the same single IrModule.liveOpIndices walk over every live op, so DFE's marginal cost per unit of program converges on a pass already known linear; a superlinear term would make that ratio GROW, not shrink. This is a stronger statement than the byte ratios alone (x2.03/2.48/2.14/2.14/2.11 vs sugarGate's x2.11/2.50/2.15/2.15/2.11), which are both just the documented Array grownCapacity ~x2.1 linear reading. (c) By construction: indexFunctionsByName is F hash inserts; seedRoots is one O(1) lookup per pendingRdataReloc; markLiveFunction marks BEFORE pushing so no function can be pushed twice and the worklist pops at most F times; markCalleesOf walks liveOpIndices(f) once per LIVE function, so O(total live ops); every callee name resolves through ONE stdlib Map.get (open addressing, cached hashes) instead of a scan; getFunc/funcCount are array indexing. Total O(F + ops + relocs + total callee-name bytes) with no nested scan, no findFirst in a loop, and no unbounded re-push. CORPUS CAVEAT, load-bearing: this is the worst case for cost and the best case for benefit. --log=ir:debug reports dfe: dropped 0 unreachable function(s) of 60 at rung 0 -- the arithmetic corpus contains NO dead code, so every allocation above is pure walk and NONE of it is offset by back-end work the prune removed. The instrument structurally cannot show the win. BENEFIT, measured separately on a heap program the corpus cannot express (Array create/push/get plus print, which pulls in the all-or-nothing installArrayRuntime closure): 15 of 31 functions dropped, and the whole compile falls 41,540->24,356 allocations (-41.4%) and 1,912,683->1,141,518 bytes (-40.3%); regalloc 24,646->11,040 allocs (-55%), isel 5,670->3,363 (-41%), encode 2,825->1,781 (-37%), prologueEpilogue 540->258 (-52%); emitted code 7,354->4,103 bytes (-44%). DFE's own cost on that program is 131 allocations, so it spends 131 to save about 17,300 -- roughly 130x. THE ONE OPTIMIZATION MADE: sweepUnreachedFunctions materialized String.from(func.name) EAGERLY beside its lazy logTrace, spending one String per function DROPPED -- a cost that rises with exactly how well the prune works -- to render a line that is off on every build but a --log=ir:trace one. Moved inside the closure: -15 allocs / -720 bytes on the heap program (-10.3% of the pass's own footprint, one String per drop at 48B), exactly 0 on this corpus because nothing here is dropped, trace output unchanged (15 lines under ir:trace), emitted code byte-identical. NOT DONE, with reasons. No compactOps/compactBlocks after the sweep: it would add an O(ops) walk plus an ops-sized forwarding array to the one column this instrument CAN see, to buy a reduction in peak resident memory, which nothing here measures at all -- and nothing downstream of DFE walks the Std module's flat arrays (verified: every Std consumer goes through liveOpIndices or func.blockRefs), so the orphans are inert, not a correctness risk. No CHUNK-level pruning (a sleep-only program still carries the __gt_trampoline chunk as dead bytes): the hand-assembled chunks are a FIXED set, O(1) in program size, so they cannot bend any curve, and pruning them needs a Target-tier chunk reachability graph -- a SECOND decider beside RuntimeUsage, which is the precise defect this rung removed. MEASURED DEBT; the trigger that would make it worth paying is chunk count becoming a function of program size, which it has never been. (1) THE IMPLEMENTER'S READING REPRODUCED, AND THE LOAD-BEARING HALF PROVEN: +68 allocs / +2,376 bytes vs row 336, bit-identical at rung 0 AND rung 5. What makes that a CONSTANT rather than a leak shaped like one is WHICH axes the ladder doubles underneath it: the corpus grows from 19 files / 12 `type` declarations at rung 0 to 143 files / 260 declarations at rung 5, and the delta does not move by one allocation. That refutes two suspects outright — `createEnumRegistry` is O(1) PER COMPILE and not O(files) (a per-file seed would have grown 7.5x), and `requireTypeNameNotCompilerOwned` -> `isCompilerOwnedTypeName` (three `ByteArray.equals`, run at EVERY `type`/`enum`/typealias declaration) allocates exactly ZERO (a 21.7x growth in declarations moved nothing). The +68 is the two enum registries' first `Map` upsert initializing their 16-slot arrays, as claimed. (2) THE CORPUS IS BLIND TO THIS RUNG, so the +68 measures only the seed. Its manifest declares `generics` NOT GENERATED, and the emitted corpus contains ZERO interfaces, ZERO enums and ZERO generic instantiations — so NONE of the witness/ordering paths run: not `builtinOrderingEnum` per operator, not `orderingTagOf`, not `findWitnessDispatch`, not the witness-reloc-gated `int.compare` install. I therefore measured them on a PURPOSE-BUILT doubling ladder (a `Pair uses T where T is Comparable` with N methods x 4 ordering operators, 64 -> 2,048 operators, against an identical-shape CONTROL on a plain concrete int). BOTH ARE LINEAR: allocs x1.809 x1.893 x1.942 x1.970 x1.984 (converging on x2.00 from below exactly as the real corpus does, the fixed per-compile constant amortizing away), bytes x2.013 at the top rung. The ordering path's OWN marginal cost — ladder minus control — is also linear (x1.844 x1.910 x1.951 x1.974 x1.986), settling at 99.7 allocations per ordering operator. No superlinear term exists in the rung. (3) `builtinOrderingEnum()` COSTS EXACTLY 10 ALLOCATIONS / 376 BYTES PER CALL, and `emitOrderingVerdict` calls it once per ordering operator. Measured directly by an A/B probe compiler that made ONE EXTRA call per operator: +5,120 allocs over 512 operators and +20,480 over 2,048 — 10.000 per call both times, +376 bytes both times (the record, three column arrays, three empty payload arrays, three first-push buffers). LEFT ALONE DELIBERATELY, and the reason is the interesting part: it is 10% of the path's 99.7 allocs/operator, it is a CONSTANT FACTOR not a curve, it is ZERO on the real corpus (shv2's own source contains no ordering operator on a constrained type parameter), and the obvious fix is a TRAP — an eagerly-built per-`Parser` cached layout would pay 10 allocations per FILE whether or not the file compares anything, converting today's FLAT +68 into a GROWING term (+1,430 at rung 5's 143 files). A lazy memo would avoid that and buy a constant factor on a path nothing takes; not worth an option field and a liveness question. `orderingTagOf` -> `indexOfCase` is a 3-element scan and allocation-free; noise, measured and left. (4) THE FIND, and it is TIME-ONLY so this instrument STRUCTURALLY CANNOT SEE IT (the corpus manifest warns of exactly this class in its own words: "a cost that re-walks tokens or chases pointers but allocates little will read FLAT in these columns even when it is quadratic in time"). `Parser.requireInterfaceForParse` (Parser.maxon:16750) resolves a `where`-constraint interface by LINEAR SCAN over `self.artifact.interfaces` after missing the builtin table — so witness dispatch is O(dispatch sites x interfaces declared in the same file). FILED AS MEASURED DEBT, NOT FIXED, because the real corpus bounds it: the maximum interfaces declared in ANY one file across maxon-shv2, stdlib, maxon-selfhosted and specs-shv2 is 17 (and `requireInterfaceForParse` scans only the CURRENT file's artifact), so K <= 17 and the term is linear-in-practice today — the `SplitLiveRanges` K^2 case, max K = 8. (2) THE QUADRATIC THIS PASS FIXED, measured on a closure-dense program the corpus cannot express (one straight-line function holding C capturing closures, each passed down to a callee that only calls it — all ACCEPTED, so the whole body is scanned): the rung as merged scanned the body ONCE PER CLOSURE, and since a function's op count grows WITH its closure count that is a quadratic in the function's own size. phase:semanticCheck BEFORE: 1.1 / 4.3 / 16.4 / 66.7 / 271.8 ms for C = 40/80/160/320/640 — x3.9, x3.8, x4.1, x4.1 per doubling, i.e. ~4x = QUADRATIC (271.8/1.1 = 247x over a 16x input; 16^2 = 256). AFTER: 0.2 / 0.3 / 0.7 / 1.7 / 2.8 ms — ~x2 per doubling = LINEAR, confirmed to a 6th rung at C=1280 (7.2 ms, x2.1). 97x faster at C=640. ATTRIBUTION IS DIRECT, not inferred: commenting out the single `checkCapturingClosureUses` call took the BEFORE ladder to 0.1/0.3/0.5/1.4/2.3 ms, so the pass WAS 99.2% of semanticCheck's time at C=640. THE FIX: `EscapeScanSubject` stops naming the KIND of value and starts naming WHICH values one pass tracks — `everyCapturingClosure` sweeps the whole marked column in a SINGLE body walk (the mark is an O(1) array read, so testing "is this operand tracked" costs the same as comparing one id), making the cost O(ops) whatever the closure density. The ONE band-append `match` that is the rung's whole guarantee is untouched and still the only enumeration of escape sinks. A `type` is no better; `ConstValue`'s own comment says so ("a `type` (so, one heap object per operand folded)"). The shipped version keeps every per-op/per-operand leaf answering in a BOOL and constructs the union at most ONCE per scan, recovering WHICH closure with a separate `oneCapturingClosure` query over the ONE op that stopped the sweep — O(closures), on the refusal path only, never on the accepting one. The parser builds SSA on the fly at a cost of O(vars-in-scope) per branch -- each `if` SNAPSHOTS the whole mutable-var set, each `while` MINTS A PHI PER VAR IN SCOPE -- so the true cost is O(V x B). But `longFunction` and `deepBlocks` each declared ONE accumulator and mutated it in every branch: V=1, O(V x B) collapsed to O(B), and the knobs NAMED for growing branches could not see the cost of growing branches. Per user directive, FIX THE CORPUS'S REALISM, NOT THE PROBE: both knobs now carry SIX mutable locals (a fixed, realistic count -- real functions carry a handful) and each branch mutates a ROTATING SUBSET, never all of them, because a branch that writes every local makes "cost O(vars in scope)" and "cost O(vars assigned)" the same number and the corpus cannot tell apart the two things that decide whether a write-trail is worth building. deepBlocks' two arms now write DIFFERENT locals, so the merge does real work instead of the trivial one-phi case. Also: every guard is now a SHORT-CIRCUIT `and`, because the corpus contained `and`/`or` EXACTLY NOWHERE -- found the hard way when the `const` flags fix moved zero memory. EAGER minting is what makes those snapshots true, and once minting is eager the criterion is ASSIGNMENTS, not mentions -- a var the loop never assigns keeps its pre-loop value, whose definition DOMINATES the header, so a read binds it correctly with no phi at all. A shift by a count the compiler cannot see now emits 6-7 ops (an and/neg/or/sar mask, then a select or a count-clamp), and the corpus has NO dynamic shifts -- so the guard contributes exactly zero here, and a CONSTANT count in 0..63 (every x shr 8, every x shl 3) is folded and pays nothing at all. StructLayout.indexOfField (Project.maxon:224) is a LINEAR SCAN, and the two references disagree about it: v1 hashes (fieldByName as FieldNameIndexMap, Project.maxon:3973, built per StructType.create) and the C# bootstrap scans (st.Fields.FirstOrDefault(f => f.Name == fieldName), 2-Parser.cs:19216). shv2 matches the bootstrap. It IS O(F) per access and it IS visible under torture - a controlled experiment holding the program shape fixed at 200 accesses and varying ONLY the field index (a scan is the one thing that can tell first from last) measures reading the LAST field of a 512-field struct at +2,658,300 ns over reading the FIRST, with the gap growing in F. But it is NOT a growth curve in program size, and the measurement that settles it is the field-count distribution of the two real codebases: v1 is 3.5x larger than shv2 (369 declared types vs 107) yet its MEAN field count is identical (5.4 vs 5.3) and its MEDIAN is LOWER (3 vs 4). Programs grow by adding TYPES, not by widening them, so the cost is O(accesses x 5.3) - linear with a bounded constant - and a per-layout name->index map would buy a heap Map per declared type plus a name hash per lookup to save ~2.6 byte-compares. That is a structural argument backed by two independent 100k+ line corpora, NOT the "a program's X count is a handful" appeal that row 141 correctly convicted layOut of - there globals genuinely grow with program size, here fields per type measurably do not. ⚠⚠ AND FOR THE SIXTH CONSECUTIVE RUNG THE CORPUS IS BLIND TO WHAT LANDED, and this time the manifest says so in its own words: "NOT GENERATED ... structs". What this row measures is EXACTLY what a struct-free program pays for the rung EXISTING, which is the 5 x files + blocks + functions + 26 above - and that is worth having (it is the proof that `return 42` still compiles to what it always did, which is the rung's own stated promise) but it says NOTHING about the heap. (3) To stay allocation-flat a consolidated value MUST be a packed scalar: a struct value heap-boxes per entry in shv2 (structs are heap values), which would turn the flat +8/rung alloc delta into +O(functions)/rung, strictly WORSE — the measured flatness proves the current scalar value is stored inline. Bit-packing the result-class bit with the param mask reintroduces a bit-layout that two readers must agree on, and forces the map type through about 11 sites in the F2a return path (buildValueClassColumns then buildFunctionValueClasses then recordBlockOpClasses then recordOpResultClass then calleeResultClass, plus both backends create, callResultRegister and threading) which the F2b brief fenced off and which is byte-pinned — invasive for a ~0.008% (one of two hash tables) saving. Rejected the sparse-map option: a missing entry degrading to an all-GPR default turns a genuinely-wrong float argument from a specific panic at calleeParamClass into a later, vaguer emitter failure. The row-over-row deltas the log shows (+133/+221/+397/+749/+1453/+2861) are vs the P1.6-C-OPT baseline row; ~+29 of the rung0 gap is the intervening P1.6-C decay/review fixes (e65a164c9, 1430ad6a0), NOT P1.5. LINEAR: row-delta ratios 1.66->1.80->1.89->1.94->1.97 -> 2.0; the true-delta split is phase:signatures +83/+151 (x1.82) and phase:parse +19/+23 (x1.21), every allocation ~48 B (empty-array-sized). SOURCE: the sole common-path allocation P1.5 adds is the 11th per-file array PendingFnMergeCheckArray in FileParseArtifact.create — allocated once in the signature-sweep throwaway artifact (queryProgramSignatures builds one Parser/FileParseArtifact per file and discards all 11 working arrays, exactly as it already discards the 10 pre-existing siblings) and once in the real-parse artifact — plus 2 one-time arrays (Project.create, resetMergeTargets). CORPUS-BLINDNESS: ScaleCorpus has ZERO ternary/match value-merges and ZERO function typealiases, so the whole-program drain, the PendingFnMergeCheck records, and FunctionTypeAlias.display are ALL UNEXERCISED here; their Big-O is by READING. TASK-2 (COW vs fresh): pendingFnMergeChecks stays FRESH-per-file, matching its 10 FileParseArtifact.create siblings (funcSignatures/funcReturnTypes/rangeChecks/typeAliases/functionTypeAliases/...), NONE of which are COW'd. It is NOT a Parser.create sharedEmpty* COW anchor: those anchors are populated only for rare generic files and were moved to module globals last pass; this array is likewise populated only for rare merge files, but COW'ing it alone while its siblings stay fresh is exactly the inconsistency the file's pattern forbids ('do NOT COW something its siblings don't'). DRAIN O-ANALYSIS (checkValueMergeSignatures, Queries.maxon): per recorded merge, per arm — armFnTypeResolvable = funcSignatures.contains OR functionTypeAliases.contains (both Map with ByteArray keys, O(1)); armFnTypeDisplay = Map.get O(1) + formatFnTypeDisplay O(params). Total O(Sigma arms x params) = O(program), NOT O(merges x functions); commitPendingFnMergeChecks is O(Sigma checks) with no file^2 loop; no whole-program scan nested in the drain. buildFunctionDisplay: the 'fn(...) returns ...' display is built ONCE per function typealias on the REAL parse (readTypeAliasDeclaration buildFunctionDisplay:true at Parser.maxon:3450) only; the token sweep (recordScannedTypeAlias, :3069) and readNestedRangedAlias (:3688) pass false — no double-build. NOTED, not fixed (constant factor on a corpus-unexercised path, not the growth mandate; flagged to reviewer): readFunctionTypeAlias (Parser.maxon:3837) allocates paramTypes MaxonTypeArray unconditionally even for buildDisplay:false, though the :3835 comment claims the false path 'pays for neither the array nor the string' — still O(function-typealias count) = linear. | 2026-07-22 | P1.0d.4 residual slices A+C MERGED (float literals + math intrinsics). SLICE A — exact decimal->IEEE-754 float literals. parseFloatBits applied the decimal exponent by repeated *10 in an i64, which WRAPPED: 1.0e20 gave a wrong value, 1.0e64 silently became 0.0 (10^k contains 2^k, so k>=64 is exactly 0 mod 2^64), and 1.0e63 HUNG THE COMPILER FOREVER (10^63 == 2^63 == i64.min, negative, so the shiftUp normalization loop doubled a negative without bound). NOT A PORT: v1's NumberParsing.maxon:13-226 is BYTE-IDENTICAL to shv2's and carries the identical bug (shv2 inherited it by copying); maxon-sharp sidesteps it via .NET double.Parse. Replaced with bounded-width base-2^32 limb magnitudes; significand accumulated in 9-digit strides to a 768-significant-digit cap plus a STICKY flag feeding round-to-nearest-even; MaxBigLimbs=144 enforced by a panicking requireLimbBudget; subnormals and underflow-to-zero added (NEITHER EXISTED); overflow now decided by the CONVERTED VALUE, not a decimal estimate. COST +1,106/+2,194/+4,370/+8,722/+17,426/+34,834 rung0..5 — exact doubling per rung, LINEAR in program size, attributed exactly: rung 0 holds 278 float literals (--emit-corpus) and 1106/278 = 3.98, i.e. an exact conversion needs two heap magnitudes where the wrong i64 pair needed none, at 2 allocations per Array (Self{} envelope + first push's ensureCapacity). 0.30% of rung-0 allocations, 0.33% of rung-5's; all phase ratios unchanged x2.00-x2.11; NOT a new complexity class. The 768+sticky correctness fix itself is +2 allocs/+80 bytes FLAT at every rung. NO small-value fast path (it would be a second implementation of one fact) and none is needed: allocations are FLAT from 1 to 34 significant digits because a magnitude's width tracks its VALUE (empty array == zero), and 91% of the 1,402 float literals in shv2+stdlib carry 2-3 digits — ~1.9ms of a ~13s self-build (0.015%). Independent optimizer: NO superlinearity, NO code change. It verified the implementer's bigShiftLeft fix (a whole-limb insert(0,..) loop, Theta(shift x count)) by REVERTING it — real on allocations (-3 allocs/-696 bytes per literal) but A WASH on wall time, because Array.insert delegates to a memmove builtin (stdlib/Array.maxon:282) while the replacement is a bounds-checked element loop; kept because it is asymptotically right and allocates less, but it did NOT buy the time it appears to. REJECTED WITH MEASUREMENTS: hoisting pow10 out of bigMulPow10's stride loop (-0.9%..-3.1% wall, ZERO allocation change, and identical code read 79,368 then 70,215 us — a 13% load swing, so the gain is BELOW THE NOISE FLOOR of the only instrument that can see it); factoring 10^k = 2^k*5^k to fold 2^k into the binary exponent (would cut operand width 1.43x and the dominant divide loop ~30%, but it is a constant factor that rewrites the exponent bookkeeping the brand-new subnormal/underflow paths depend on — bit-risk against freshly-settled correctness); Knuth algorithm D for the 53-step mantissa loop; fusing bigCompare+bigSubtract. Worst admitted input is 768 digits at e-300 (~350-400 us/literal), NOT 1000 digits at e-324 (201 us) — the latter falls below the least subnormal, significandBits clamps to 0, and the divide loop runs ZERO iterations. INDEPENDENT REVIEW FOUND A BLOCKER THE SUITE WAS GREEN OVER: sciExp was clamped at a FIXED 100000, but it competes with decExp in magnitudeExp = (sigDigits-1)+decExp+sciExp, and decExp moves ONE PER DIGIT — so a long enough digit run OUT-PULLS the clamp and FLIPS THE VERDICT IN BOTH DIRECTIONS. 0.<200001 zeros>1e999999999 (true magnitude 1e999799997, astronomically past f64.max) was ACCEPTED AND COMPILED TO 0.0 with no diagnostic; 1<200000 zeros>.0e-999999999 got a SPURIOUS E2011. Both verdicts confirmed against the bootstrap oracle. The ceiling is now bytes.count()+DecimalWindowSpan, swept over 96 cases spanning the cancellation region, 0 disagreements, with two regression tests PROVEN to have teeth (reintroducing the fixed clamp fails exactly those two). Review also DEDUPED the carry flush, written THREE times (bigAddSmall, bigMulSmall, and a third spelling in bigShiftLeft that pushed UNMASKED and deferred the budget check) with nothing making them agree — folded into bigPushCarry so requireLimbBudget's claim that every growing site ends there is STRUCTURAL, not conventional. And it CORRECTED THE 768 BOUND'S JUSTIFICATION: the comment claimed midpoints terminate within 752 digits, but 752 is the width of 5^1075 ALONE, ignoring the odd significand which runs to 2^54-1 and adds up to 16 more — the true widest rounding boundary (2^54-1)*2^-1075 needs EXACTLY 768, so the budget has ZERO slack while its stated reason would have justified shaving 16 digits off it (the same wrong number appeared in the spec prose: one fact written down twice, wrong both times). Two further stale bounds that contradicted each other (341 vs the real 1091) corrected; the extremum literal reaches exactly 114 limbs against the budget of 144, measured by narrowing MaxBigLimbs and binary-searching, so requireLimbBudget CANNOT fire on admitted input. MEASURED DEBT FILED WITH ITS TRIGGER: bigMulPow10 and the digit accumulation are Theta(strides x limbs) — quadratic in exponent and digit count — LINEAR IN PRACTICE only because the real corpus tops out at 21 significant digits and the only extreme-exponent literals are a handful of type bounds. The trigger that would bend it is A WIDER FLOAT TYPE: binary128 pushes the decimal exponent 308->4932, the digit budget 768->~11,500 and MaxBigLimbs 144->~1,700, growing both quadratic terms ~15x per dimension (~225x). A shared enum makes both copies spell the same member NAMES; it does not make the MAPPING shared. Change ceil to towardNegative in one isel and NEITHER SITE READS AS WRONG: x64 silently computes floor where the program said ceil — a WRONG ANSWER, not a build break — and asymmetrically so, because the x64 copy is pinned by committed goldens while arm64's can be regenerated on NO host available here, so the weakly-gated copy is the one free to drift. The FLAT +77 allocs (and the ~+4,728 writeExe bytes) are a SEPARATE fixed per-compile cost: kernel32ImportNames iterates IatSlot.allCases, so the 15 new IOCP kernel32 imports are written into EVERY PE unconditionally — a constant, not a scaling term. DECISION: ACCEPTED as inherent LINEAR debt (the flat-StdOp thesis tax: pay max-case width for O(1) dispatch), NOT narrowed. ReadFile is genuinely a 5-argument call, and trimming osReadOverlapped to 5 operands needs a contortion — pack the NON-ADJACENT bytesOut (a per-read slab alloc) and ovl (embedded in the GT struct at 0x60) behind a hard-coded backend layout offset, or hard-code the buffer cap, or fork the op — none clean or correctness-neutral, and the anti-dup reviewer would collapse a fork. +8 B/StdOp is a constant factor, linear in program size, no bent curve (mandate: constant factors are not the target; growth curves are). RE-MEASURE TRIGGER: the first future StdOp case with >=7 associated values pushes maxPayload 6->7 and re-widens every StdOp instance by a further 8 B; until then every <=6-field op rides free. IO PATH superlinearity hunt: NONE — the io runtime graph is usesIocp-gated (never emitted for the arithmetic corpus) and is a fixed-size emission; the runtime proc/handle store scans are cap-64 bounded. (b) The body/sugarGate cost ratio DECREASES monotonically, 1.195->1.135->1.099->1.066->1.041->1.024 for allocs and 1.084->1.027 for bytes. sugarGate is the same single IrModule.liveOpIndices walk over every live op, so DFE's marginal cost per unit of program converges on a pass already known linear; a superlinear term would make that ratio GROW, not shrink. This is a stronger statement than the byte ratios alone (x2.03/2.48/2.14/2.14/2.11 vs sugarGate's x2.11/2.50/2.15/2.15/2.11), which are both just the documented Array grownCapacity ~x2.1 linear reading. (c) By construction: indexFunctionsByName is F hash inserts; seedRoots is one O(1) lookup per pendingRdataReloc; markLiveFunction marks BEFORE pushing so no function can be pushed twice and the worklist pops at most F times; markCalleesOf walks liveOpIndices(f) once per LIVE function, so O(total live ops); every callee name resolves through ONE stdlib Map.get (open addressing, cached hashes) instead of a scan; getFunc/funcCount are array indexing. Total O(F + ops + relocs + total callee-name bytes) with no nested scan, no findFirst in a loop, and no unbounded re-push. That row credited a "+16K..+246K x2.0/rung `moduleReferencesWhitelisted` short-circuit scan" as the whitelist's last growing term and called it linear-but-minimal. It was neither the scan nor minimal: the scan is ALLOCATION-FREE (a direct `block.opRefs` walk that allocates nothing). The growing term was the O(functions) reachability MAP — the name->index map + `reached` bool array the runtime-floor skip's full walk builds — which built on EVERY compile because the short-circuit NEVER FIRED: the whitelisted set is internally connected (`Clock.elapsedMs` calls `Clock.nowMs`), so scanning the whitelisted bodies THEMSELVES always found an intra-whitelist edge and returned true. The prior "bypass A/B" that isolated +16K..+246K bypassed the WHOLE function, so it measured (free scan + always-built map) and mislabeled the sum "the scan". THE FIX skips `whitelisted.contains(func.name)` bodies before scanning, so the only edge that counts is the first crossing INTO the whitelist from a non-whitelisted (user/runtime) caller — the exact question — which is false for the whole non-Clock corpus, so the map is now never built. ## Exponents — CLOSED 2026-07-14. Kept as history; no longer written.

**The tool no longer fits exponents, and no longer records time at all.** The row below is the only
one that was ever machine-written, and it is left exactly as it was measured. Deleting honestly-taken
numbers to tidy up a format change would be the worst of both worlds; they are simply not extended.

Two reasons it went, and they are the same reason twice:

**The ladder DOUBLES, so the ratio between two rungs already IS the growth.** x2.00 is linear, x4.00
is quadratic — read straight off the allocation counts in the tables above, no fit required. An
exponent could tell you nothing those numbers had not already told you, and it dragged in a residual,
which dragged in a NOISY verdict, which got an exemption written for it so it would stop complaining.
That is optimizing the gauge instead of the engine.

**And time can never be trended.** It is machine-dependent, so a dated column of it compares a loaded
box in July against an idle one in August. The `time /` half of every cell below is a fact about the
machine that ran it. (The compiler still times itself — `--log=compiler:debug` and `--metrics=<path>`
report per-phase milliseconds for one compile, which is where a clock is worth reading.)

What replaced it: `scale-test` prints per-phase **allocations and bytes at every rung**, with the
rung-over-rung ratio beside them. `regalloc:liveness` reads **x2.80** and `regalloc:splitting`
**x2.52** where every other phase sits on **x1.98** — the known-superlinear splitter, visible without
a single fitted number.

## Notes on the changes

The four earliest rows predate the automated log; their numbers are reconstructed from the diffs in
git, so they are accurate but were not written by the tool. They also predate the exponent table,
which is why it starts empty.

**`667ec9eee` — a name is a slice of the source, not a heap String.** By far the largest win so far.
Token text stopped being a heap `String` and became a zero-copy `ByteArray` slice into the source
bytes already in memory. Note the *shape*: rung 0 fell 14% but rung 5 fell 43%. That did not shave a
constant factor, it bent the curve.

**`4f81c7efe` — a byte string literal IS a ByteArray, and may be a global.** A bootstrap fix, not a
compiler optimization. `b"..."` at module scope resolved to an auto-created `__Array_i8` rather than
the stdlib `ByteArray`, which forced byte constants to be rebuilt per occurrence inside accessor
functions. The measurable win is small; the point was making the lexer's keyword table a byte-literal
map at all.

**`ac9e7db47` — for-in over an Array is an index counter.** `for x in arr` no longer allocates an
iterator object (a heap `ArrayIterator` wrapping a heap cursor). It lowers to an integer counter over
the backing buffer, in the parser, where the loop is built — replacing a post-monomorphization pass
that pattern-matched the CFG back into a loop and bailed on exactly the loops a compiler actually
runs (arrays of ops, blocks, tokens, strings).

**`EnumDummy` — a `try_call` on an associated-value enum stops allocating a placeholder.** The
biggest single win in the table: −24.6% of *all* allocations at rung 5. The bootstrap lowered a
throwing call returning a union into a heap-allocated dummy enum, a `select` between it and the real
result, and a decref of whichever lost — because a `try_call` returns null on the error path and the
lowering believed scope cleanup needed a real rc=1 allocation to decref. It never did: scope-end
cleanup already emits a null-GUARDED decref, and every managed slot is zeroed on function entry, so a
null slot is already the well-defined "nothing to release" case. On the success path the dummy was
allocated, increffed, decreffed and freed **without ever being read**. Null is what absent means, and
storing it is now the whole lowering.

The dummy was also *leaking* on the error path (see below), so this is a correctness fix wearing an
optimization's clothes.

**2026-07-16 — bootstrap constant resolver, not shv2: no table row moved, and none could.** This
change is in the **C# bootstrap's** cross-file top-level constant resolver, which `scale-test` does
not measure — the instrument compiles generated programs with **shv2**, and shv2 was rebuilt
byte-for-byte identically by the patched bootstrap (sha256 `1062b0d…`, forward *and*
`MAXON_SOURCE_ORDER=reverse`). The subject of every row above is unchanged, so there is nothing for
the tool to record. (On this arm64-macOS host the instrument cannot run at all: shv2's M1 backend
allocates for x64-windows only, so `RegisterAllocator.maxon` panics on the host arch — orthogonal to
this change.)

The bootstrap regression it fixes was real and superlinear. The rung `2408411dc` made cross-file
constant resolution order-independent by folding every reference against a **whole-program-visible**
list of constant declarations; `EvaluateConstant` looked a name up in that list with a linear
`FirstOrDefault`, once per reference — **O(references × constants)**, quadratic in a constant-heavy
project. Measured on a generated corpus of N cross-file constants with `--timing` (the bootstrap's
own per-phase instrument, since `scale-test` measures the wrong compiler here), the `preScan` phase
grew **×4.2 per doubling** at N = 2000→4000 (39 → 164 ms) — a clean quadratic. Replacing the scan
with a name→decl dictionary built once per file (first-wins, so a file's own declaration still
shadows a foreign one of the same name) makes each lookup O(1): that same step is now ×2.4 (15 → 36
ms) and the dominant term is gone. The O(files × constants) that remains is the *same* class as the
pre-existing `SeedFromModule` seed this diff never touched — sub-millisecond at the ~600-constant
scale of a real self-build, and quadratic before this rung too.

**2026-07-17 — the perspective-fold's cache was PER-PARSER, so a foreign perspective was rebuilt in
every parser that read it. Bootstrap again; no table row moved.** The follow-up `d7ed60d30` folds each
top-level constant in its DECLARER's perspective, resolving that file's private aliases; a foreign
perspective is built by `ConstantDeclSetFor(filePath)`, which scans the whole-program declaration list
once and memoizes the result. The memo was an **instance field** (`_constantDeclSetCache`), and a fresh
`Parser` is created for every file in every pass — so the SAME declarer's perspective was rebuilt once
per parser that folded one of its constants, across both the PreScan and Parse passes. On a deep
cross-file constant chain folded in a seeding-defeating order (which is exactly what
`MAXON_SOURCE_ORDER=reverse` exercises) the cascade re-derives each perspective at every level of every
parser: total perspective-build work grew **super-quadratically**, above the pre-existing and accepted
O(files × constants) baseline.

Measured with a temporary deterministic counter (perspective builds, and decls scanned across them —
exact, load-independent) on a generated chain of N single-constant files, `MAXON_SOURCE_ORDER=reverse`:
builds grew **×2.5–3 per doubling (~N¹·³)** and total decls scanned **×~4.5 (~N²·¹⁶)** — F, the number
of distinct perspective demands, was *larger than the file count* because the cache did not dedupe
across parsers. The fix threads ONE cache through every parser of a compilation
(`Compiler.CompileSources` owns it; scoped to the call, not static, so a reused module — the LSP's
cached stdlib module — never serves a stale set). After it: builds are **exactly linear (×2.00 per
doubling, one build per file)** and decls scanned is **×~4 = O(N²)**, the baseline class restored. At
N = 3200 that is 71.1M → 10.4M decls scanned (**6.85×** less), and reverse-order `preScan` wall-clock
went from **4.56× the parent at N = 3200 to 1.97×** — a bounded constant factor over the parent's own
O(files × constants), where before the gap *widened* with N.

It was a **latent** superlinearity, not a live regression: on realistic input the whole path is inert —
the stdlib (~600 constants) does **0** perspective builds and a real `maxon-shv2` self-build does **2**
(816 decls scanned), because real cross-file constant references are shallow and mostly fold from a
value already seeded by their declarer. Behaviour is byte-identical: **3041/3041** C# spec tests pass
forward AND `MAXON_SOURCE_ORDER=reverse`, `git status specs/ specs-shv2/` empty (codegen unchanged),
the shv2 self-build is bit-for-bit `1062b0d…` in both orders, and the three perspective/collision
reopen behaviours hold both ways (`cross-file-exported-reads-own-private` → 42,
`cross-file-exported-cast-to-own-private-alias` → 42, `cross-file-private-constant-name-collision` →
**122**, not 42). No run exited 101. (`scale-test` measures shv2 and cannot run on this arm64-macOS
host anyway — the bootstrap's `--timing` plus the temporary build counter are the right instruments
here, as with `0c974245e` above.)

**2026-07-18 — the arm64 encoder stopped building a `RegisterFile` per register operand.**
`scale-test` could not measure this: its corpus is heap/struct/global-heavy and the arm64 M1 backend
panics at `osAllocPages` on rung 0, so the ladder never produces arm64 data on this host. Measured
instead with `--metrics` on a synthetic 200-function scalar module (~36,800 ALU statements, which the
arm64 backend *can* emit), before vs after:

| phase | allocs before | allocs after | bytes before | bytes after |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| encode | 123,130 | 10,913 | 2,636,989 | 841,517 |
| total | 1,527,795 | 1,415,376 | 115,908,333 | 114,109,634 |

**−112,217 allocations in the encode phase (−91%), and the total delta (−112,419) is the same number**
— so essentially every allocation removed was in the encoder. The cause: `arm64RegNumIsGpr` (the class
test every colored operand goes through, via `arm64GprField`/`arm64FpField`) read the GPR/SIMD boundary
as `arm64RegisterFile().firstVector`, **constructing a throwaway `RegisterFile` heap object to read the
compile-time constant 31** — once per register operand of every op, i.e. O(ops) pure-waste heap
objects. The x64 side never had this: `regClassOf` reads the `FirstXmmRegisterNumber` constant directly
and its comment says it does so precisely to avoid building a `RegisterFile` on the encoder's
per-register path. The fix names the arm64 twin (`Arm64FirstVectorRegister = 31`), so `arm64RegisterFile()`
and the encoder share one home for "31" and the hot path allocates nothing (the two non-hot per-function
readers, `arm64CallerSavedMask` and `arm64UsedCalleeSaved`, read the constant too). It is a constant-
factor win, not a bent curve — the count is still linear in ops — but the constant was ~110k objects a
scalar module. Codegen is byte-identical (the module emits the same 154,464 code bytes before and after;
`git status specs-shv2/fragments/` empty, so both the x64-windows and arm64-macos goldens are
untouched); the arm64 suite is **489/0**, exit 0 (no leak). x64 is provably neutral — nothing on the x64
path was touched and `arm64RegisterFile()` still returns the same value.

## Bugs this uncovered

Removing the dummy exposed a leak it had been half-hiding, and the leak in turn exposed a hole in the
test suite. Both are fixed; both are worth knowing about.

**A routed try-block call leaked one reference per call.** A bare throwing call inside a
`try 'blk' … end 'blk' otherwise (e)` block has its result hoisted into a `__try_block_result_N` temp
that receives the callee's *transferred* reference — so that temp owns it and must release it.
`VarRegistry.KeysSince` excluded exactly those temps from scope-end cleanup, on the theory that a
downstream `let x = <call>` aliased the same slot without increfing. That was true for a struct
return, which was separately handed a `CallReturn` `__call_tmp_` that turned the alias into a *move*,
and false for an associated-value union, which was handed nothing and so increfed like any other
alias. Both special cases are gone: the try-block form now works the way every single-statement `try`
form already did, and the temp that receives the reference is the temp that releases it.

**80% of the spec suite could not see a memory leak.** `mm_leak_check` overrides the process exit code
with 101 when an allocation is still live at exit. But 2311 of the 2886 tests are compiled together
into one batched binary whose per-test verdicts are parsed from stdout markers carrying each test's
own `main` return value — and `TestRunner` discarded the batched binary's process exit code entirely.
The leak checker ran, printed, and had its verdict thrown away. A leaking batched test still emitted a
full set of passing markers. The runner now reads that exit code; because the leak counter is
process-global and cannot say *which* test leaked, a non-zero batch exit invalidates the batch and
re-runs its tests individually, where each one's own leak check attributes it.

## What the profile says is left

From `scale-test --per-type`, which attributes every allocation to the TYPE allocated *and* the SCOPE
that allocated it. **The scope column is the one that finds things** — a `String` row can never tell
you that 150 of them came from `emitFixedToken`.

| what | share | note |
| --- | ---: | --- |
| `OpIndexList` / `BlockRefList` | ~17% | `IrBlock.opRefs` and `IrFunction.blockRefs` are **linked lists of integers** — a heap node per op reference, plus a `ListIterator` per traversal. `Array` would kill all three (nodes, list boxes, iterators). Now the biggest single win available. |
| `stepBackwardOverOp` et al. | ~7% | A superlinear cluster inside the register allocator (fitted exponent ~1.47), not a constant factor. |

`ArrayIterator` (once the single biggest allocating scope, at 102,845) and `EnumDummy` (once ~20% of
all allocations) no longer appear.
